I saw its running lights, green and red, and its white lantern hanging from the great forestay. A vague reflection lit up its rigging and showed that the fires were being forced to the utmost. Showers of sparks and cinders of burning coal, escaping from its funnels, starred the air.

I stayed there until six in the morning without Captain Nemo seeming to notice me. The warship was a mile and a half from us, and with the first light of day its cannonade began again. The moment could not be far off when, as the _Nautilus_ attacked its adversary, my companions and I would leave forever this man I did not dare to judge.

I was getting ready to go below to warn them when the first officer came up onto the platform. Several sailors were with him. Captain Nemo did not see them, or did not want to see them. Certain arrangements were made that might have been called the _Nautilus_’s “clearing for action.” They were very simple. The handrail that formed a railing around the platform was lowered. Likewise, the beacon housing and the helmsman’s cage were drawn back into the hull until they were just flush with it. The surface of the long sheet-iron cigar no longer offered a single projection that could hinder its maneuvering.

I went back to the drawing room. The _Nautilus_ was still on the surface. A few gleams of morning were seeping into the layer of water. Under certain undulations of the waves the windows came alive with the red glow of the rising sun. That terrible day of June 2 was dawning.

At five o’clock the log told me that the _Nautilus_ was slowing down. I understood that it was letting itself be approached. Besides, the detonations were growing louder. The cannonballs were plowing the surrounding water and boring into it with a strange whistling.

“My friends,” I said, “the moment has come. A handshake, and may God keep us!”

Ned Land was resolute, Conseil calm, and I nervous, hardly able to contain myself.

We went into the library. Just as I was pushing open the door onto the well of the central staircase, I heard the upper hatch close sharply.

The Canadian rushed for the steps, but I stopped him. A well-known hissing told me that water was entering the ship’s tanks. Indeed, in a few moments the _Nautilus_ submerged a few meters below the surface of the waves.

I understood its maneuver. It was too late to act. The _Nautilus_ did not intend to strike the two-decker in its impenetrable armor, but below its waterline, where the metal plating no longer protects the planking.

We were prisoners once more, forced witnesses of the grim drama that was being prepared. Besides, we hardly had time to think. Taking refuge in my room, we looked at one another without saying a word. A profound stupor had taken hold of my mind. The movement of thought stopped within me. I was in that painful state that comes while waiting for a dreadful explosion. I waited, I listened, I lived only by the sense of hearing!

Meanwhile the speed of the _Nautilus_ increased noticeably. It was gathering momentum. Its whole hull was quivering.

Suddenly I cried out. There was a shock, but a relatively light one. I felt the penetrating force of the steel spur. I heard scraping and grating. But the _Nautilus_, carried along by its propelling power, passed through the mass of the warship like a sailmaker’s needle through canvas!

![His gaze seemed to draw it on.](imagens/im110.jpg)

I could not stand it. Mad and beside myself, I rushed out of my room and ran into the drawing room.

Captain Nemo was there. Mute, somber, implacable, he was looking through the port panel.

An enormous mass was sinking under the waters, and so as to lose nothing of its death agony, the _Nautilus_ was going down into the abyss with it. Ten meters from me I saw that gaping hull, into which the water was rushing with a sound of thunder, then the double row of guns and the bulwarks. The deck was covered with black shadows moving about.

![The enormous warship was slowly sinking.](imagens/im111.jpg)

The water was rising. The poor wretches rushed into the shrouds, clung to the masts, writhed under the water. It was a human anthill caught by an invading sea!

Paralyzed, stiff with anguish, my hair on end, my eyes wide open, my breathing cut short, without breath, without voice, I too was watching! An irresistible attraction held me glued to the window!

The enormous warship was slowly sinking. The _Nautilus_, following it, was watching its every movement. Suddenly there was an explosion. The compressed air blew up the vessel’s decks as if the magazines had caught fire. The thrust of the water was such that the _Nautilus_ swerved.

Then the unfortunate ship sank faster. Its tops appeared, laden with victims, then its crosstrees, bending under clusters of men, and finally the top of its mainmast. Then the dark mass disappeared, and with it that crew of corpses dragged down by a formidable eddy....

I turned toward Captain Nemo. That terrible avenger, a true archangel of hatred, was still watching. When it was all over, Captain Nemo went to the door of his room, opened it, and went in. I followed him with my eyes.

On the far wall, below the portraits of his heroes, I saw the portrait of a woman, still young, and two little children. Captain Nemo looked at them for a few moments, stretched out his arms to them, and, kneeling, broke into sobs.

## XXII. CAPTAIN NEMO’S LAST WORDS {#cap-2-22 .chapter}

The panels had closed on that frightful vision, but the light had not been restored in the drawing room. Inside the _Nautilus_ there was nothing but darkness and silence. It was leaving that place of desolation, a hundred feet under the water, with prodigious speed. Where was it going? North or south? Where was this man fleeing after that horrible reprisal?

I had gone back to my room, where Ned and Conseil stayed silent. I felt an insurmountable horror of Captain Nemo. Whatever he had suffered at the hands of men, he had no right to punish like that. He had made me, if not the accomplice, at least the witness of his revenge! That was already too much.

At eleven o’clock the electric light came back on. I went into the drawing room. It was deserted. I consulted the various instruments. The _Nautilus_ was fleeing north at a speed of twenty-five miles an hour, sometimes on the surface of the sea, sometimes thirty feet below it.

When our position had been plotted on the chart, I saw that we were passing the mouth of the English Channel and that our course was carrying us toward the northern seas at incomparable speed.

I could hardly catch, as they rushed past, the long-nosed sharks, hammerhead sharks, and spotted dogfish that frequent these waters, great eagle rays, clouds of sea horses like the knights in a game of chess, eels wriggling like the serpent squibs of a fireworks display, armies of crabs fleeing sideways with their pincers crossed over their shells, and finally schools of porpoises racing the _Nautilus_. But there was no longer any question of observing, studying, or classifying.

By evening we had crossed two hundred leagues of the Atlantic. Darkness came, and the sea was covered in shadows until the moon rose.

I went back to my room. I could not sleep. I was assailed by nightmares. The horrible scene of destruction kept repeating itself in my mind.

From that day on, who can say how far the _Nautilus_ carried us in that basin of the North Atlantic? Always at an incalculable speed! Always amid the hyperborean fogs! Did it touch the points of Spitsbergen, the steep shores of Novaya Zemlya? Did it travel those unknown seas, the White Sea, the Kara Sea, the Gulf of Ob, the Lyakhov Islands, and those unexplored shores of the Asian coast? I cannot say. I could no longer reckon the time that was passing. The hour had been suspended on the clocks aboard. It seemed that night and day, as in the polar regions, no longer followed their regular course. I felt myself carried into that realm of the strange where the overwrought imagination of Edgar Poe moved at ease. At every moment I expected to see, like the fabulous Gordon Pym, “that veiled human figure, of far vaster proportions than any inhabitant of the earth, thrown across the cataract that guards the approaches to the pole!”

I estimate, though I may be mistaken, I estimate that this adventurous course of the _Nautilus_ went on for fifteen or twenty days, and I do not know how long it would have lasted but for the catastrophe that ended this voyage. Of Captain Nemo there was no longer any sign. Of his first officer, no more. Not a man of the crew was visible for a single instant. Almost continually the _Nautilus_ floated under the water. When it rose to the surface to renew its air, the panels opened or closed automatically. No more positions marked on the planisphere. I did not know where we were.

I will also say that the Canadian, at the end of his strength and patience, no longer appeared. Conseil could not get a single word out of him, and feared that in a fit of delirium, under the sway of a terrible homesickness, he might kill himself. So he watched over him with devotion at every moment.

It will be understood that under these conditions the situation was no longer bearable.

One morning, on what date I cannot say, I had dozed off in the first hours of the day, a painful, sickly doze. When I woke, I saw Ned Land leaning over me, and I heard him say to me in a low voice:

“We are going to escape!”

I sat up.

“When do we escape?” I asked.

“Tonight. All watchfulness seems to have vanished from the _Nautilus_. You’d say a stupor reigns aboard. Will you be ready, sir?”

“Yes. Where are we?”

“Within sight of land that I made out this morning in the fog, twenty miles to the east.”

“What land is it?”

“I don’t know, but whatever it is, we’ll take refuge there.”

“Yes, Ned! Yes, we’ll escape tonight, even if the sea swallows us!”

“The sea is rough and the wind strong, but twenty miles in that light boat of the _Nautilus_ doesn’t frighten me. I’ve managed to stow some provisions and a few bottles of water in it without the crew knowing.”

“I will follow you.”

“Besides,” added the Canadian, “if I’m caught, I’ll defend myself; I’ll get myself killed.”

“We will die together, friend Ned.”

I was ready for anything. The Canadian left me. I went up onto the platform, where I could barely keep my footing against the shock of the waves. The sky was threatening, but since land was there in that thick fog, we had to escape. We must not lose a day or an hour.

I went back to the drawing room, at once fearing and wishing to meet Captain Nemo, wanting and no longer wanting to see him. What would I have said to him? Could I hide from him the involuntary horror he inspired in me? No! Better not to find myself face to face with him! Better to forget him! And yet!

How long that day was, the last I was to spend aboard the _Nautilus_! I stayed alone. Ned Land and Conseil avoided speaking to me for fear of giving themselves away.

At six o’clock I dined, but I was not hungry. I forced myself to eat in spite of my distaste, not wanting to weaken myself.

At half past six Ned Land came into my room. He said to me:

“We won’t see each other again before we leave. At ten o’clock the moon won’t have risen yet. We’ll take advantage of the darkness. Come to the skiff. Conseil and I will be waiting for you there.”

Then the Canadian went out without giving me time to answer.

I wanted to check the course of the _Nautilus_. I went to the drawing room. We were running north-northeast at a frightening speed, at a depth of fifty meters.

I cast a last look at those wonders of nature, at those riches of art heaped up in this museum, at that unrivaled collection destined to perish one day at the bottom of the sea with the man who had formed it. I wanted to fix one last impression in my mind. I stayed there an hour, bathed in the glow of the luminous ceiling and reviewing those treasures shining in their cases. Then I went back to my room.

There I put on sturdy sea clothes. I gathered my notes and fastened them carefully on my person. My heart was pounding. I could not slow its beating. Certainly my agitation and distress would have betrayed me to Captain Nemo’s eyes.

What was he doing at that moment? I listened at the door of his room. I heard footsteps. Captain Nemo was there. He had not gone to bed. At every movement it seemed to me that he was going to appear before me and ask why I wanted to escape! I felt constant alarms. My imagination magnified them. This feeling became so acute that I wondered whether it would not be better to go into the captain’s room, see him face to face, and defy him with gesture and look!

It was a madman’s inspiration. Fortunately I held myself back, and I lay down on my bed to calm the agitation of my body. My nerves settled a little, but, my brain overexcited, I saw again in a rapid memory my whole existence aboard the _Nautilus_, all the happy or unhappy incidents that had crossed it since my disappearance from the _Abraham Lincoln_: the underwater hunts, Torres Strait, the savages of Papua, the running aground, the coral cemetery, the Suez passage, the island of Santorini, the Cretan diver, Vigo Bay, Atlantis, the ice bank, the South Pole, the imprisonment in the ice, the fight with the devilfish, the storm in the Gulf Stream, the _Vengeur_, and that horrible scene of the warship sunk with its crew!... All these events passed before my eyes like backdrops unrolling at the back of a theater stage. Then Captain Nemo grew beyond all measure in that strange setting. His figure sharpened and took on superhuman proportions. He was no longer my fellow man; he was the man of the waters, the genie of the seas.

It was then half past nine. I held my head in both hands to keep it from bursting. I closed my eyes. I no longer wanted to think. Another half hour of waiting! A half hour of a nightmare that could drive me mad!

At that moment I heard the vague chords of the organ, a sad harmony beneath an indefinable melody, the true laments of a soul that wants to break its earthly bonds. I listened with all my senses at once, hardly breathing, plunged like Captain Nemo into those musical ecstasies that carried him beyond the limits of this world.

Then a sudden thought terrified me. Captain Nemo had left his room. He was in the drawing room, which I had to cross to escape. There I would meet him one last time. He would see me; perhaps he would speak to me! A gesture from him could annihilate me, a single word chain me to his ship!

Meanwhile ten o’clock was about to strike. The moment had come to leave my room and join my companions.

There was no room for hesitation, even if Captain Nemo should rise up before me. I opened my door cautiously, and yet it seemed to me that it made a frightful noise as it turned on its hinges. Perhaps that noise existed only in my imagination!

I crept along the dark gangways of the _Nautilus_, stopping at every step to slow the beating of my heart.

I reached the corner door of the drawing room. I opened it gently. The drawing room was plunged in deep darkness. The chords of the organ were sounding faintly. Captain Nemo was there. He did not see me. I even believe that in full light he would not have noticed me, so completely was he absorbed in his ecstasy.

I dragged myself across the carpet, avoiding the slightest bump whose noise might have betrayed my presence. It took me five minutes to reach the door at the far end, which opened onto the library.

I was about to open it when a sigh from Captain Nemo nailed me to the spot. I understood that he was getting up. I even glimpsed him, for a few rays from the lighted library were filtering into the drawing room. He came toward me, arms folded, silent, gliding rather than walking, like a ghost. His heaving chest was swelling with sobs. And I heard him murmur these words, the last that ever reached my ear:

“Almighty God! Enough! Enough!”

Was it the confession of remorse escaping like this from the conscience of this man?...

Beside myself, I rushed into the library. I climbed the central staircase and, following the upper gangway, reached the skiff. I went into it through the opening that had already let my two companions through.

“Let’s go! Let’s go!” I cried.

“At once!” answered the Canadian.

The opening cut into the sheet iron of the _Nautilus_ was first closed and bolted with a monkey wrench that Ned Land had brought with him. The opening of the skiff was closed as well, and the Canadian began to unscrew the nuts that still held us to the underwater boat.

Suddenly a noise was heard inside. Voices were answering one another excitedly. What was happening? Had our escape been noticed? I felt Ned Land slip a dagger into my hand.

“Yes!” I murmured. “We will know how to die!”

The Canadian had stopped his work. But one word, repeated twenty times, a terrible word, revealed to me the cause of the agitation spreading through the _Nautilus_. It was not us the crew was after!

“Maelstrom! Maelstrom!” they were crying!

The Maelstrom! Could a more frightening name, in a more frightening situation, have rung in our ears? Were we, then, in those dangerous waters off the Norwegian coast? Was the _Nautilus_ being dragged into that abyss just as our skiff was about to break away from its side?

It is well known that at the turn of the tide the waters squeezed between the Faroe and Lofoten Islands rush out with irresistible violence. They form a whirlpool from which no ship has ever been able to escape. Monstrous waves come running from every point of the horizon. They form that abyss rightly called the “Navel of the Ocean,” whose power of attraction reaches a distance of fifteen kilometers. Not only ships are sucked in there, but whales, and also the polar bears of the northern regions.

It was there that the _Nautilus_ had been steered by its captain, involuntarily, or perhaps deliberately. It was describing a spiral whose radius grew smaller and smaller. Like it, the skiff, still fastened to its side, was carried along at dizzying speed. I could feel it. I felt that sickly whirling that follows too long a spinning motion. We were in terror, in horror carried to its height, our circulation suspended, our nerves numbed, drenched in cold sweat like the sweat of death! And what a noise around our frail skiff! What roarings, repeated by the echo for several miles! What a crash of those waters breaking on the sharp rocks of the bottom, where the hardest bodies are shattered, where tree trunks are worn down and grow “a fur of bristles,” as the Norwegian expression has it!

![The skiff flung into the middle of the whirlpool.](imagens/im112.jpg)

What a situation! We were tossed about horribly. The _Nautilus_ was defending itself like a human being. Its steel muscles were cracking. At times it reared up, and we with it!

“We have to hold on,” said Ned, “and screw the nuts back on! If we stay attached to the _Nautilus_, we can still save ourselves...!”

He had not finished speaking when there was a cracking sound. The nuts gave way, and the skiff, torn from its socket, was flung like a stone from a sling into the middle of the whirlpool.

My head struck an iron rib, and under that violent blow I lost consciousness.

![I was lying in a fisherman’s hut.](imagens/im113.jpg)

## XXIII. CONCLUSION {#cap-2-23 .chapter}

Here is the conclusion of this voyage under the seas. What happened during that night, how the skiff escaped the formidable eddy of the Maelstrom, how Ned Land, Conseil, and I came out of the abyss, I cannot say. But when I came to, I was lying in the hut of a fisherman of the Lofoten Islands. My two companions, safe and sound, were beside me and pressing my hands. We embraced one another warmly.

At the moment we cannot think of returning to France. Means of communication between northern Norway and the south are rare. So I am forced to wait for the steamer that makes the twice-monthly run to the North Cape.

So it is there, among the good people who took us in, that I am going over the account of these adventures. It is accurate. Not one fact has been omitted, not one detail exaggerated. It is the faithful narrative of this improbable expedition into an element inaccessible to man, whose routes progress will one day make free.

Will I be believed? I do not know. It matters little, after all. What I can now affirm is my right to speak of these seas, under which, in less than ten months, I have traveled twenty thousand leagues; of this underwater tour of the world that has revealed so many wonders to me across the Pacific, the Indian Ocean, the Red Sea, the Mediterranean, the Atlantic, and the southern and northern seas!

But what has become of the _Nautilus_? Did it withstand the grip of the Maelstrom? Is Captain Nemo still alive? Is he still carrying out his frightful reprisals under the ocean, or did he stop after that last hecatomb? Will the waves one day bring the manuscript that contains the whole story of his life? Will I at last learn this man’s name? Will the vanished warship, by its nationality, tell us Captain Nemo’s nationality?

I hope so. I also hope that his powerful machine has conquered the sea in its most terrible abyss, and that the _Nautilus_ has survived where so many ships have perished! If so, if Captain Nemo still inhabits the ocean, his adopted country, may hatred be appeased in that fierce heart! May the contemplation of so many wonders put out the spirit of vengeance in him! May the avenger fade away, and may the scientist continue the peaceful exploration of the seas! If his destiny is strange, it is also sublime. Have I not understood that for myself? Have I not lived ten months of that unnatural existence? And so, to the question asked six thousand years ago by Ecclesiastes, “Who has ever been able to fathom the depths of the abyss?” two men among all men now have the right to answer: Captain Nemo and I.
